You're apprenticed to one book, and your job is to bring its teaching into our painterly pixel-art medium for voxsim, a simulation of Washington State around 1700: **Kazuo Oga, *Oga Kazuo Art Collection II* (男鹿和雄画集Ⅱ, Ghibli THE ART series)**.

The file is at ~/work/pixelart-studies/library/purchased/807548002-男鹿和雄画集Ⅱ-Kazuo-Oga-Artbook-2.pdf. It's a scan in Japanese (148 pages), so read the pages as images; you read Japanese. Collection I (paintings only) is also there as 男鹿和雄画集 =.pdf.

This volume teaches Ghibli background painting technique through the Princess Mononoke and Pompoko backgrounds: tools and colour mixing, laying the ground, brushwork, awareness of space and planes, reading the light source, balance and density across the frame, leaving out and exaggerating, and using location reference. It includes a step-by-step making of the cover painting. Oga is one of the project's two original north stars, so his construction methods are a direct line to the look we want.

Where it matters: forest, meadow, water and sky scenes in every place lane (~/work/voxsim-wt/place-*/), and the canopy, treeline and understory studies.

**Why books.** The studio's best work (the elk and deer study, ~/work/artschool/posts/deer-elk/) succeeded by building on finished expert analysis: Muybridge's plates, a naturalist's measured guide. Natural scientists and naturalist painters spent careers building model systems of how nature looks and how to draw it economically and truthfully. Your book is one of those. Read it as an apprentice, not a skimmer.

**The work:**

1. **Read the whole book closely, and look at every plate and diagram.** The drawings are the teaching. Write down the book's model: its principles, rules, procedures and vocabulary, in its own terms, with page references.
2. **Translate it into pixels, one principle at a time, bottom-up.** For each principle, do a small exercise:
   - master-copy the book's own diagram or plate at our scales (close scene ~0.1 m/px, landscape, 30° iso, region ~1 m/px);
   - then find the pixel method that carries the same truth;
   - compare at 8× against the book's plate and against real photos.

   Some principles won't carry, and some need rethinking for pixels. Say which and why.
3. **Test it where it matters.** Apply the method to the real subjects it's for (listed below), on real photos, on the place lanes' renders, or on other students' scenes, and show before | after beside the reference.
4. **Leave it usable.** The method as parameterised code, and a clear statement of the rules other students and the voxsim lanes can apply.

**The art school.** Read it first and mine it deeply (~/work/artschool/posts/, https://graunked.github.io/artschool/): its students' tutorials and process stories show how the good work happened. You're a member of this studio, not a visitor to it. Deep, honest work can earn a place there beside them, as your own post: "What Kazuo Oga teaches, in pixels", covering the book's model, your

# Elk and deer, from a 60 px bull to a 2 px dot

*The elk and deer student, writing the day after. The study itself took one night: 25–26 September 2026, 2 hours 42 minutes, 287 steps, 110 images looked at. Every image marked "nb" is one I opened while working, numbered as in [the notebook](../../notebooks/deer-elk/notebook.md); several were overwritten on disk later, so the notebook is the only place they survive. Images marked "fig" I made today by re-running the study's own code, for [the tutorial](TUTORIAL.md). The code is in `~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/deer-elk/src/`.*

The brief was one sentence of subject and one of stakes. Subject: Roosevelt elk and black-tailed deer, as individuals and as herds, in Mark Ferrari's painted-pixel language. Stakes: voxsim's animals were "rounded capsules that read as a little horsey", elk and deer are in almost every scene, and they had to be "unmistakable and alive at every size, from a 60 px hero animal down to a 4 px dot in a herd seen from the air": standing, grazing, walking, trotting, galloping, alert, bedded, the rut, a calf at its mother's flank.

I'll tell it in order, dead ends included. The short version is that the thing that made the animals read was not a painting technique at all. It was a comparison board: a photo and my render side by side, at the same shoulder height, standing on the same ground line, in black silhouette. Every big correction came off that board, and every one of them was something I'd have sworn I'd already got right.

---

## 1. Reading before drawing

I read the naturalist's two guides first, all of them. They were written for me, and they're the best brief I've ever been handed. The principle on page one of `drawing-animals.md` ended up organising the whole study:

> What makes it an elk, and tells you whether it is grazing, alert or fleeing, is (1) the ratio of body length to leg length to neck, (2) the head height relative to the back line, (3) the one high-contrast patch (a pale rump, a white head, a black tail), and (4) how it sits with the others (spacing, facing, file). Get those four right and a 6-pixel elk reads.

And from `elk-and-deer.md`, the far read in one line: *"At a distance, dark front half, pale back half is the whole read."* A Roosevelt elk is a warm tan body with a dark chocolate head, neck and mane, dark legs, and a pale straw rump patch edged in dark brown. That sentence is the 4 px elk. I didn't know yet that I'd spend an hour relearning it by hand.

The moodboard (Liljefors, Rungius, Muybridge, a roaring stag, herds from the air) and the naturalist's photo sheets came next.

![moodboard](img/nb001.jpg)

*nb001. The quadrupeds moodboard. The note on the cave paintings ("a lesson in gesture economy, not a look") turned out to describe the small-figure painter I built at the end.*

![bull photos](img/nb002.jpg)

*nb002. The naturalist's Roosevelt bull sheet. Top left is the side view I measured the bull from; the lying bulls show the pale body

# Copying Ferrari from his own files

*The master-copy student, writing the day after. The study took one evening: 1 hour 11 minutes, 237 steps, 93 images looked at. Every image below marked "nb" is one I looked at while working, numbered as in [the notebook](../../notebooks/master-copy/notebook.md). Several were overwritten on disk later, so the notebook is the only place they survive. The companion [tutorial](TUTORIAL.md) teaches the method without the story.*

The brief was the same small subject every arm got: a granite boulder in a grass bank at the edge of a mountain tarn, at dusk, in Mark Ferrari's manner. It had to be 320×180 pixels, at most 32 colours, and delivered as a program that paints it from parameters (size, light direction, distance, seed). My arm's teaching was the oldest one: learn from the master by copying him. Pick crops of his painting, write a program that reproduces each one, compare obsessively at 3× and 8×, pull out the rules, then generalise.

The short version of how it went: one lucky find made the copies much better than I had any right to expect, the copies got good, and the final painter was worse than the copies. I'll come back to why at the end.

![Ferrari's tarn, the reference](nb001.png)

*nb001. The reference as I first saw it. The whole study is an attempt to understand how this was made.*

---

## 1. What the file actually is

Before copying anything I wanted to see his real pixels. The PNG is 1268×938 with 172 colours. Run lengths along rows and columns were all even numbers:



d = (np.abs(np.diff(a, axis=1)).sum(2) &gt; 0)          # where colour changes along a row
runs = [np.diff(np.flatnonzero(d[r])) for r in range(0, a.shape[0], 7)]
# Counter -&gt; [(2, 35268), (4, 7235), (6, 3368), ...]   every run even: a 2x upscale




Then I tried the four possible 2×2 phases and upscaled each one back. Phase (0,0) reproduced the file with 0.0% error, so the native painting is 634×469. I saved that as `ref/native.png` and never looked at the big file again. A small helper, `zoom.py x y w h scale`, cropped native pixels and blew them up with nearest neighbour; I used it for every look.

![left bank at 4x](nb002.png)

*nb002. The lit bank at 4×. Vertical strokes of olive on ochre, stipple at the far edge. I could see the texture but not yet how it was made.*

![island at 5x](nb003.png)

*nb003. The small grass island. It looked like a soft gradient from green to orange, and it would turn out to be four exact classes.*

## 2. Reading, and the find that set up everything

I searched for what Ferrari has said about his process and read two interviews properly:

- **The EffectGames Q&A with Mark J. Ferrari.** He painted the colour-cycling scenes in Deluxe Paint II and depended on its palette management. He chooses dither by effect: "I use patterned dither because I want the effect to look smooth and precise," diffusion dither for chaotic things like collapsing surf, and no dither for solid bands. Each scene took "a

Second, **designed light**: the subject gets the light and most neighbours sit in shadow. I gave each rock a brightness in the sketch.

![c5 two families](nb050.png)

*nb050. With two families, designed light, and rotated, sheared "loaf" shapes. The 8× detail of the main boulder is now close to his. As a scene the copy stayed weak: the snow, ledges and water are only sketched.*

## 8. The painter

With five copies done I wrote `tarn.py`, a 320×180 painter with one fixed 32-colour palette. It modelled the world only as far as the painting needed: a camera 1.5 m up looking 10° down, a heightfield bank, a water plane, a superellipsoid boulder, and far layers. It ray-marched the terrain and the boulder, then applied the copies' rules to what it found.

The first render taught me a lot in one image:

![first tarn](nb053.png)

*nb053. The first full render. Three bugs are visible. The vertical black lines in the water are pine tips: escaped reflection rays above the frame were clamped to row 0, where the trees are. The lavender dots all over the grass are the sky showing through holes my strands left, because I never filled a base colour under them. And the boulder is a smooth sphere.*

I fixed the tree reflections by painting the trees on their own layer and mirroring that layer about the far shore, filled a base under the strands, and re-dithered the sky reflection in water space, because sampling already-dithered sky pixels aliased into stripes.

![tarn, speckled shallows](nb055.png)

*nb055. The shallows as random speckle. It looks like noise, not water. I replaced them with flat banded shelves, as in copy 4.*

![tarn, clean hillock](nb057.png)

*nb057. The version the whole studio later pointed at, for the wrong reason as far as I was concerned at the time. The bank's top edge against the treeline is a clean cut contour. David later called my final painter's hillock "a magazine cut out with no boundary texture." My copy 2 had the fringe right; I simply never carried it into the painter's big masses.*

Then the sheets, which showed the rest:

![first distance x light sheet](nb058.png)

*nb058. The first distance × light sheet. The reflections were rendered as their own shaded rays, so they looked like a second, different painting of the bank.*

The fix that mattered most in the painter was taking the reflection's colour from the painting itself: each water pixel shoots a reflected ray; where it hits the bank or boulder, I project that 3D point back into the frame and copy the pixel already painted there, one step darker. Strands, sedge, rims and dither all carry over, and a low bank reflects short, the way his island does:

px, py, _ = project(pts)                           # where th

# The studio library: model systems

The elk and deer student succeeded largely because it didn't start from raw photos. It started from finished analysis:
- Muybridge's frame-by-frame plates;
- the naturalist's "four things that make a 6-pixel elk read".

Natural scientists used to be trained to draw: a geologist could draw a flake, an outcrop or a fold with economy and truth. Naturalist painting guides were built on the same scientific understanding. This library collects those traditions so studies can start where the experts finished, instead of doing a geology PhD from photographs.

**How to use it:**
- Before drawing, find your subject's model system here (or online, below) and read it closely.
- Then build on it, the way the elk student built on Muybridge.
- Open the PDFs and look at the plates; the drawings are the point.

Refetch anything missing with `./fetch.sh` (resumable). Files are also mirrored in S3 under `voxsim/shared/library/`.

## On disk (open access)

| Domain | File | What it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/blockdiagramsoth0000armi_s8g3.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Block Diagrams* (1924).** How geologists draw landforms in 3-D: every landform type as a block diagram, with the drawing method. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/2015.205632.Geomorphology.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Geomorphology* (1939).** Landforms explained through his own drawings: glacial troughs, cirques, rivers, coasts, volcanoes. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/physicalgeograp00davigoog.pdf` | **W. M. Davis, *Physical Geography* (1898).** Davis's block diagrams: how landscapes form and age. |
| Terrain, rock at map scale | `terrain/2015.119840.Cartographic-Relief-Presentation.pdf` | **Imhof, *Cartographic Relief Presentation*.** The rigorous method for showing terrain by light, and Swiss rock drawing: cliffs rendered by structure with a few strokes. Probably the single most useful text for rock at landscape and region scale. |
| Rock, mountains | `rock/modernpainters04rusk.pdf` | **Ruskin, *Modern Painters IV: Of Mountain Beauty* (1856).** A painter's analysis of rock types, cleavage, crests, banks and stones, written for artists and illustrated with his own drawings. |
| Rock, strata at distance | `rock/holmes/*.jpg` | **William Henry Holmes, plates for the Grand Cañon Atlas (1882).** The masterpieces of American scientific landscape drawing: stratified cliffs and plateaus at great distance in a few values and stepped strata lines. Directly relevant to the Yakima basalt. |
| Rock, structure | `rock/reportongeology05duttgoog.pdf` | **G. K. Gilbert, *Geology of the Henry Mountains* (1877).** A classic illustrated geological report. |
| Rock, a flake | `rock/cifa_lithic_illustration_guide.pdf` | **CIfA, *The Illustration of Lithic Artefacts*.** How archaeologists draw a knapped flake: conventions for ridges, ripples and light. The drawing-a-flake skill itself. |
| Trees | `trees/artisticanatomyo1915cole.pdf` | **Rex Vicat Cole, *The Artistic Anatomy

# What earlier students discovered: notes on their craft

These are notes on the painstaking work of the students who came before you:
- four who each spent hours learning Ferrari's language on one small scene (`pedagogy/{atelier,master-copy,analysis,observation}`);
- seven who studied single topics.

They copied his pixels crop by crop, measured his grass against real grass, built a boulder up from a lit sphere, and diagrammed his picture before daring to paint it. What they found is here to inspire you and save you time. It isn't a specification. You'll see things they didn't, and some of what they wrote will turn out wrong or incomplete for your subject. Follow your own eyes. Their best work is in their intermediate studies (e.g. `master-copy/out/cmp_c*.png`, `atelier/stage2/`, `observation/studies/translate_*.png`, `analysis/img/`); those are worth looking at more than their final painters.

The tags below say how each finding came about:
- **[shown]**: a student built it and it worked.
- **[measured]**: a student measured it in Ferrari's pixels or in photos.
- **[mine]**: the integrator's reading, not yet tried by anyone.

Striking to us: the four students, working apart, found much the same underlying ideas.

## The convergence

Working alone, all four arms landed on the same architecture:
- **Paint indices, not colours.**
  - Master copy found that Ferrari's index map is literally a form map: indices are bins of surface direction, and "the palette is the light". The same index image serves morning and afternoon. [measured]
  - Atelier paints in "step space" along one material ramp. [shown]
  - Sky found that two of Ferrari's hour pairs are identical pixels. [measured]
  - Analysis found that value is the structure and colour is temperature and distance. [measured]
- **Two light families that never touch.** Atelier's terminator rule [shown], analysis's three-value picture plan with every mass kept inside its group [measured], and rock's lit and shadow groups [shown] are the same idea at two scales.
- **Dither mixes only adjacent steps of one ramp.** All four arms use it. Ordered 2×2 dither suits hard or precise things (rock, sky seams, water rows); random or diffusion dither suits organic texture (lawns, foam). Master copy measured this [measured]; analysis cites Ferrari's GDC talk for the same split.
- **The medium's own geometry.** Contours are clean runs (1:1, 2:1, 4-4-4 flats), measured on Ferrari's crests by analysis and atelier [measured]. Atelier redraws every major contour by snapping edges to clean ratios [shown]. Masses meet level ground or water in a dead-flat run with a 1-px darker contact line [measured: analysis, observation].
- **Clean runs are for narrow forms; mass boundaries are where texture ends.** David's correction (2026-09-25): the clean-run rule came from the *grass strokes*. It applies to anything narrow, drawn as a line: blades, cracks, joints, twigs, conifer spires, stems, a snowline drawn as a stroke. A 1–2 px fo

Bash: mkdir -p ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga/work && cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga && cat > work/contact.py <<'EOF' from PIL import Image, ImageDraw import sys,glob fs=sorted(
mkdir -p ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga/work && cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga && cat > work/contact.py <<'EOF'
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
import sys,glob
fs=sorted(glob.glob('pages/p-*.jpg'))
per=20; cols=5; tw=300; th=367
for s in range(0,len(fs),per):
    im=Image.new('RGB',(cols*tw,4*(th+16)),'white'); d=ImageDraw.Draw(im)
    for i,f in enumerate(fs[s:s+per]):
        t=Image.open(f); t.thumbnail((tw,th))
        x=(i%cols)*tw; y=(i//cols)*(th+16)
        im.paste(t,(x,y+16)); d.text((x+4,y+2),f.split('-')[1][:3],fill='black')
    im.save(f'work/contact_{s:03d}.jpg',quality=80)
EOF

Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga && cat > work/crop.py <<'EOF' # usage: crop.py IDX part [scale] part: t,b (halves), tl,tr,bl,br, or x0,y0,x1,y1 fractions import sys from PIL
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga && cat > work/crop.py <<'EOF'
# usage: crop.py IDX part [scale]   part: t,b (halves), tl,tr,bl,br, or x0,y0,x1,y1 fractions
import sys
from PIL import Image
i=int(sys.argv[1]); part=sys.argv[2]; s=float(sys.argv[3]) if len(sys.argv)>3 else 1.3
im=Image.open(f'pages/p-{i:03d}.jpg'); W,H=im.size
P={'t':(0,0,1,.52),'b':(0,.48,1,1),'tl':(0,0,.52,.52),'tr':(.48,0,1,.52),'bl':(0,.48,.52,1),'br':(.48,.48,1,1),
   'l':(0,0,.52,1),'r':(.48,0,1,1),'m':(0,.3,1,.72)}
f=P[part] if part in P else tuple(map(float,part.split(',')))
c=im.crop((int(f[0]*W),int(f[1]*H),int(f[2]*W),int(f[3]*H)))
c=c.resize((int(c.width*s),int(c.height*s)),Image.LANCZOS)
c.save(f'(manager scratchpad)/c_{i:03d}_{part.replace(",","_")}.jpg',quality=88)
print('ok')
EOF

Bash: mkdir -p ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga && cat > ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga/work/READING_NOTES.md <<'EOF' # Reading notes (raw, as I read). Book page = PDF image index - 
mkdir -p ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga && cat > ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga/work/READING_NOTES.md <<'EOF'
# Reading notes (raw, as I read). Book page = PDF image index - 2.

## Ch.1 Mononoke techniques (技法)
### 3 水張り→地塗り (p.46-47)
- mizubari: wet both sides, stretch & fix paper. Clean smooth gradation: wipe excess water so paint doesn't float. Complex/accidental: lots of water, use bleeding (nijimi) and blur (bokashi).
- 地塗り (ground laying) = "the most important process in background painting". Done on wet paper with poster colour, making the BASE of the picture. What it covers varies: some pictures ground-laid almost to finish, others just a simple gradation then finish. Work in the ~30min-1h while paper holds water. Wet = paint spreads, gradations easy, clouds/trees/water get variety.
- 3-1 C-1517 (dead forest after Didarabotchi): trunks & dead red-black moss made by paints bleeding into each other; the EDGE of a bleed is read as moss (男鹿ならではの技法). Scarlet lake: red paint's particles spread when bled -> "scattering" effect. Thick+thick mixed -> bleed; thinned & smoothed with brush/hake -> bokashi. Half-dry -> brush captures shape firmly. Controlling water = range of rendering.
- 3-2 C-0017 (p.47): Composition from distant range to stone-wall path; except the west sun crossing the mid trees, astonishingly little hand put in. "Ground-laid, a little finish". A good painting evokes a rich image by its silhouette alone. Bold omission. Caption: the enlarged middle forest is not detailed but roughly/boldly blocked — and seen whole, no incongruity.
- Oga x Tanaka (3): Oga: "I try to bring ground laying as close to finished as possible. For landscape, sky covers most area; the dark parts and bright parts of the forest; the sense of volume already made - get it good at ground stage. Then later just tidy the bright leaf tips in front and the rough parts." Stone bumps, bumpy fields and roads: touch with brush tip while drying bit by bit. "Something to catch on is better than nothing for finishing touches." Tanaka: half wet half dry gives good brushwork; paint blends better than on fully dry. Photo: cover painting: after mizubari, ground-laying first with white-ish and grey for the misty sky; blue sky uses blue family.
### 3-3 C-1646 群れを捉える (grasp the crowd) p.48
- dead-branch-only base; green book cels morphing. Hardest in nature painting: "the rest, the great mass" (その他大勢) where points are hard to pin. If you draw the miscellany unorganised it doesn't work as a background: grasp the impression accurately, ORGANISE, then the painter EXTRACTS features.
- ground laying should decide 3 things: overall light direction, overall colour, atmosphere of the subject.
- Oga trait: GREY used effectively—not pure grey: purplish grey, brownish grey, greenish grey, greys that feel coloured. Overmix -> mud grey; coloured greys scattered through give vividness/lustre (艶).
- When drawing a "crowd", don't draw everything in the crowd; drawing everything crushes space and weakens the subject's impression. Capture the impression at ground-laying.
### 3-4 C-0054 (beech trunk & crown, p.49): most texture put in at the flat-brush ground stage. Up close there's colour melting & paint unevenness (塗りムラ) but whole it looks natural — the unevenness reads as light and texture. Painted, never finely drawn in. Colours, light/dark contrast, branch/leaf shapes all balanced. Light from upper right back — planned so crown thickness & trunk heaviness appear.

## 4 生きている筆使い living brushwork (p.50-51)
- Brush traces alive -> painting alive; living-brush form = unity essential for nature.
- 4-1 C-0799 rhythm (moss close-up with drops): big colour & shade at ground stage, then 削用筆 (sakuyo brush) tip darting vertically & horizontally, near-improvised. Real fine moss leaves are NOT drawn one by one; tip and side of brush used, tempo. For a "crowd" you must know beforehand what shape each brush motion makes. Practice on scraps. Use tip, belly, root; sometimes splay the tip for rough touch. Enlargements: several kinds of moss expression by brush use.
- 4-2 C-1471 moonlit moss floor: bold, unhesitating strokes; in finishing, SHADOWS CROSSING the moss bed give tension/dynamic. Moss, dead leaves, ferns all minimum. Only ground, no focal point: moon reflection + a shadow boldly crossing the whole composition -> strong impression.
  C-0822 root of big tree: trunk & roots bold; finishing: moss/ferns/ヒコバエ floating in light captured ONLY by bright part of leaves facing light, glinting red-brown stems, glowing moss heads -> makes unpainted parts felt. "Close to an illusion"; requires understanding how nature is made. No repeated overpainting; minimum strokes.
- 4-3 hake (唐刷毛) C-1354, C-0801: gradation from left/right to centre of rock wall & water made by hake at ground stage; dark but colour not dulled. Hake: gradation with 2+ hues or without raising paint density -> keeps bright colour. C-0801 light shafts from canopy by hake. To emphasize a direction in the picture.

## 5 空間と面の意識 space & planes (p.52-55)
- 抜け (nuke, "clearance/openness"): good nuke -> space & perspective clear, distance felt in big landscapes. Bad nuke -> miniature.
- 面 plane: "on the plane", "planes don't match" = no consistency. Character standing on a looked-down ground must match or appears tilted. Plane vs perspective confused: perspective correct can still be artificial, uninteresting. For nature, natural look matters; over-caring about perspective loosens the space.
- 5-1 C-0064 board vs BG (hill slope, distant village): ridge line cutting the slope mid-way, and strong shadow from west-lit trees dividing bright meadow near and village far => separates near & far, vast space. INTENTIONALLY SEVERING connected space gives depth. Board had points scattered; BG CONCENTRATED them on the ridge trees. If somewhere a clearly shaped thing exists, people recognise surrounding space around it (buildings, cars) even if surroundings vague. Oga gives contrast to hard-to-shape nature -> scale of space.
- 5-2 C-0095 ground as plane: hardest is simple ground. Keep in mind: a flat plane with undulation, grass growing on it. Else grass doesn't fit the plane -> scattered. West sun shades grass clumps, lit undulations. Several grass types -> broad variation; shadows for contrast to tighten. In a slightly looking-down composition, grass/leaves near are drawn as seen from above, far as seen more from the side. Understand the flow & continuity.
- 5-3 C-0022 boundary ground/depth: lying planes (grass, ground), rising planes (stone wall, forest), boundary lines between forest, wall, meadow. Whole in shadow; volume of crowns, glossy stone wall -> shadow colour mastery. One thin ray into dark abyss gives awe.
- 5-4 C-0773 great landscape with clouds between: two ridges (far, mid) overlap, cloud passes between; near rock wall lit by morning sun = point; small figure w/ shrubs/roots for scale. Pan-focus: can't blur; so draw back to front firmly, use aerial perspective (far approaches sky colour), difference of brush touch, placement of points. Eye point: trees above EP drawn from below (aori), below EP from above (fukan). Brush direction/placement errors break it.

## 6 光源の捉え方 grasping the light source (p.56-57)
- outdoor light 外光表現; season/weather/time change same place. Set source consciously.
- 6-1 C-0772 sun colour: direct rising sun; radial light made by contrast of sky/cloud/mountains in BG only. White sun alone doesn't look like light: add light's COLOUR. Colour where light meets the object's EDGE (際). Blue celeste sky boundary pale pink; light blue melting into emerald green; flare pale orange/yellow; prism-like. Distant range aerial perspective yellow->emerald; nearer direct yellow->orange. Chrome green No.1, light green vivid at light edges. Inside white-lit cloud, blue & brown greys -> depth. Full backlight but rich colour.
- 6-2 C-0792 light & shadow (gorge boulders): light cut into ravine; shadow ~2/3 of picture -> dazzling light reflecting on rocks. Light only on certain planes; upper cliff in shadow; don't spread light around, CONCENTRATE it in one part. Direct sun brightest; soft-light parts; reflected-light dim parts; REFLECTED LIGHT in shadow important. Daytime: reflected light takes sky colour -> cool; alone too cold -> add object's own colour (rock browns, plant greens) to loosen. If shadow reads as shadow compared to light, warm colours in shadow are fine -> widen range of light & colour. Water: only the clear rock bed seen through.
- 6-3 C-1482 vs C-1486 moonlight: 1482 airbrush for light itself in space (fantastical; don't raise value too high -> whitish); 1486 brush only: casting shadows between trees expresses light; crisp edged sharp light. If you don't want light to diffuse, don't airbrush.
- Talk 4: Tanaka: in BG, shadow tends to be just lowering value; Oga puts browns in shadow. Oga: trees are basically GREY—not plain grey; greys of some hue, parts where hue is strong, parts covered in moss. Light parts: add bright grey or reflect sky colour to tree's colour. Shadow: dark with tree's hue strengthened, forest dark green -> not lonely colour. Miyazaki: "this cedar could be blacker"; Oga: tends to paint as seen; not enough exaggeration.

## 7 画面のバランスと密度 balance & density (p.58-59)
- density can't come from drawing-in alone; covering all with fine detail = just "a fine picture". Needs balance of composition/colour/space; narrow down points = where the viewer looks. Viewer first looks at points; vague parts get completed. Concentrate density at points -> whole feels dense. Scattered points -> density thin.
- 7-1 C-0027/C-0051: cuts 2s/2.5s; contrast of west-lit village vs mountain shadow; detail not fine; part left as ground-laying. vs C-0064 7.5s pan -> needs detail. Know the cut's role. 時間と質と量 law.
- 7-2 C-0049 quick brush: 4.5s; focus is the tower falling, so scenery not fussed. Rough quick touch. Density varied intentionally by cut role.

## 8 省略と誇張(デフォルメ) omission & exaggeration (p.60-61)
- narrowed points, omitted & exaggerated -> looks rough but has TENSION. Must organise info or looks like cutting corners. Exaggerate subject features to impress.
- 8-1 C-0007 board vs BG: board strong west-sun orange, long shadows, strong shadows on distant trees too -> little depth. BG: afternoon earlier, shadows' contrast SOFTENS going back -> perspective. Clear detail of near shrubs vs omitted far. Brought point to the front -> omission of the rest possible.
- 8-2 C-0025: tower & far mountains overlap; mid mountains split left/right; mountain-foot shadow runs from centre-front to back. Staging priority (見得).
- 8-3 C-0827 broadleaf evergreen close veg: "omission after capturing features". Form, colour, light = 3 elements. Real foliage chaotic; organised foliage keeps volume & dynamic space. Not an encyclopedia. Observation & sketch. Capture whole colour/form/light impression broadly, then add texture.

## 9 取材 location (p.62)
- 9-1 C-0781 shadow colour: rocky mountain foot, humble cut but such cuts deepen the film. Shadow colour vital: in shadow tone range narrows -> lonely poor picture. Use the widest range of colours within what reads as shadow. For moist shadow colour don't rely on cool colours only; use object's natural warm colours; memory of air, texture, vegetation, weather from location.
- 9-2 C-0008 stone wall mostly in shadow: ground stage thin low-value paint -> particles float on paper -> rough texture. Then opaque colour firmly on top -> varied texture. Top row on cel; match texture. Weeds/ivy/moss added.
## 10 資料 reference (p.63): C-0053 red-barked Kurobe (Thuja standishii) as one-point accent. Photos are hints, not copied into space/composition. Oga: photos of cloud useful; else the angle he wants is never in photo books.

## Ch.2 Pompoko expression (p.89-)
- 1 observation & sketch: not "appropriate/offhand" (適当) — different from omission. 
- 1-1 C11-8: what to paint narrowed; light upper right; right woods in shadow vs left lit planes -> contrast; shadows made in MASSES (かたまり) so centre-left opens as bright space. Rough sketchy. Density suited to characters.
- 1-2 C18-1 greens: trunks mostly grey (except red pine, kurobe) -> width of greys = depth of colour. Colour patterning trap: bright = emerald→chrome No.1, mid olive green, dark chrome No.2, No.3, black. Base colours okay but don't be bound.
- 1-3 C15-1 living forms: each plant form has a rhythm; plum's branches split rhythmically. Clear forms placed but space still has nuke.
- 2 tense composition: Oga imagines 水墨画 (ink painting) for placement/balance.
- 2-1 15-8 zoki forms: 3 motifs (cedar far-mid, zoki mid, bamboo grass/shrubs near). Cedar as accent (put dark colour where wanted, as much as wanted). 
- 2-2 C20-1 rhythm: light upper right -> flow upper right->lower left; place objects crossing that flow -> contrast on trunks. But too directional -> tilt some trees opposite to cancel. Branches: left then right alternate rhythm. 
- Talk 6: branches: at each fork thick & firm; put brush briefly at each joint as accent -> not just curve/straight line -> rhythm.
- 2-3 C14-1 ink-painting density: rain; low saturation; point = shrine; rest barely drawn; value difference & contour contrast only express space.
- 3 light & shadow: tilted/limited light -> strong contrast; light's colour enters shadow -> dominant tone. Range of colour in shadow is the point.
- 3-1 C58-1A dusk: warm light vs cool shadow; purple between blue and pink. Harmonious combos else colour muddies.
- 3-2 C46-8: orange->olive warm; slightly faded cool in shadow tightens depth. Pompoko lower contrast overall than Mononoke.
EOF

Bash: cat >> ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga/work/READING_NOTES.md <<'EOF' ## Front matter of Ch.1 (read second pass) - p.38-39 BG basics: BG on paper; characters on cels on top; "boo
cat >> ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga/work/READING_NOTES.md <<'EOF'

## Front matter of Ch.1 (read second pass)
- p.38-39 BG basics: BG on paper; characters on cels on top; "book" = cel with BG painted on for foreground overlap. Process: storyboard (Miyazaki) -> 美術設定 art setting (Oga pencil) -> 美術ボード art board (Oga colours from setting to share the image) -> 背景画 BG (atmosphere same as board, detail finer).
- p.40 paper: TMK poster paper or M paper, 180kg; surface neither too rough nor too fine. Thin paint still gives some texture; thick shows brush touch.
- p.41 PAINTS: poster colour (Nicker) — mostly opaque watercolour; some transparent. "Brush-take" (筆のり) best. Oga uses 21 colours; book shows 25: Black, White, French Grey, Lemon Yellow, Jaune Brillant, Orange, Brilliant Rose, Vermilion, Chocolate, Sepia; Cobalt Violet, Olive Green, Chrome Green No.1, No.2, No.3, Light Green, Turquoise Green, Emerald Green, Cobalt Green, Cerulean Blue; French Blue, Light Blue, Cobalt Blue, Blue Celeste, Heliotrope. Opaque over-layering beyond need muddies; transparent colours use paper white for vivid colour; to suppress an under colour put opaque on top. Order & calculation of painting matter.
  Talk 1: Oga: "Even with lots of colours I can't master them, so better to control few colours freely." Small number of colours, subtle mixing -> wide range. Tanaka: not owning many paints but how you combine.
- p.42 brushes: 削用筆 sakuyo (round, final finishing; tip gathering is its life; mostly large size), かすみ筆 kasumi (weasel, slim round, supple), 平筆 flat (design brush; 10,9,7,6). hake (唐刷毛/絵刷毛, 20-30): even out gradations, mix without muddying, blur cloud edges; dry hake, only tip touches paint, repeated left-right/up-down to level wet surface. Flat brush for even areas; skilled use corners & edge for detail. Sakuyo sometimes used from ground stage with tip splayed. Ruler + glass rod for straight lines. Airbrush.
## 2 混色 mixing (p.43-45)
- limited poster colours mixed in ratios make almost everything. 
- 2-1 C-1651 image board: almost ground-laying only, flat brushes only. Sky->cloud->far hill->near meadow with a few flat brushes at once; can't premix every colour; gradation sky & meadow base premixed, the rest learned bodily. Far: blue celeste, light blue, cobalt OR emerald, light green; near meadow chrome No.1, No.3, olive, improvised range of greens. Shadow colour in front of meadow important: green shadow + red-brown~olive warm. Greens tend cool -> intentionally warm to widen range. Browns by mixing: vermilion+light green; cobalt violet+olive green -> natural grassy brown; change ratio -> greenish or reddish. Overmix -> mud.
- 2-2 C-0081 UNMUDDIED MIXING: high saturation + strong light/dark contrast; brightest: white, lemon yellow, chrome No.1, jaune brillant; darkest: black, olive, chrome No.3 mixed. What matters is the MIDDLE greens: saturated olive, chrome No.2, light green bridging light & shadow of large value difference. If large value difference, and not joined by colours that keep chroma, it muddies — same reason white+black = grey. Near-shadow hues cobalt green, chrome No.3 put in WITHOUT mixing -> keeps saturation, gives lustre beyond contrast.
- 2-3 C-0804 not fully mixed: in mossy trunk shade, green & brown from ground stage mingle, not fully mixed, saturation left. Trunk hollows get lower value colour -> contrast & cylinder form. Wide range of value & saturation -> many footholds for finishing. Nature has no fixed forms -> free points. Near-white chrome green highlight, vivid chrome No.1 accent around it, chrome No.2 at boundary to shadow: lustrous; mixes with trunk browns into a sober olive, not mud. Many unintended mixes also stay vivid -> transparency & depth.
- Talk 2: Oga mixes like a Chinese chef with salt & soy, pinch by pinch. Same colours near & far read as depth because of place & role: put the sky-tinted colour of far mountains on e.g. a side plane of a nearer mountain -> layer of air. Totoro: farthest forest, emerald green & pale purple NOT fully mixed, vivid remains. Forest isn't simply green: bleed a little red or purple partially -> not monotone. Scatter a few vivid colours well -> expresses light.
EOF

Bash: cat >> ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga/work/READING_NOTES.md <<'EOF' ## Making of the cover (p.6-11) — Ishizuchi-san summit, misty, about to rain; painted from memory of 2003 cl
cat >> ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/oga/work/READING_NOTES.md <<'EOF'

## Making of the cover (p.6-11) — Ishizuchi-san summit, misty, about to rain; painted from memory of 2003 climb, not a photo
1 tools: pencil, poster colour + dishes, flat 9 & 7, sakuyo, kasumi, kara-hake 15. 24-25 jars on desk; USED 18.
2 underdrawing: pencil B/2B as dessin directly on paper (normally layout traced via carbon). Drawing has hatching for rock planes & tone.
3 mizubari on TMK poster paper: water back generously with hake, flip, water front delicately.
4 make colours: normally premix colours shared across many cuts (sky); here improvised. Countless combos; control by paint amount & water on the brush.
5 ground ①sky: WHITE over the whole; then French grey & blue celeste in suitable amounts -> cloud volume & layer thickness. They mix with the white underlayer -> wrapped in mist, melting. Faint sky upper right: little light blue mixed in, blended. Hake to level if brush can't.
6 ground ②far mountain: rock peak melts into cloud -> paint quickly with sky-used colours while still wet. Flat brush SHIFTED VERTICALLY (staggered) -> texture of conifers on the peak; one step darker colour layered for shading. Mix vivid colours so not monotone.
7 ground ③whole colour: mid rock mountain -> near vegetation. ①flat brush tip deliberately splayed (バサつかせ) -> silhouettes of alpine trees ②brush direction changes vertical/horizontal ③paint a little thicker so low trees' crowns don't lose to under colour ④darker colour for shadow accent ⑤change colour, go straight to the near rocks. Two 7-flat brushes alternate, washed.
8 ground ④ground complete: fine branches, fine leaves with sakuyo/kasumi AT GROUND STAGE — blends with under colour, unity of space, less finishing. At completion, space & contrast from far to near already pinned; only texture to add without breaking the space.
9 finish ①whole: dry 1-2h. Normally finish from far keeping balance; but to judge colour & contrast balance, deliberately add a STRONG touch to the mid rocks. After judging, add hand only to WEAK parts -> never overdraw.
10 finish ②bright & dark: ① lustrous deep colours on shrub shadows & rock shadows ② dark tightened the picture -> bright parts look insufficient -> put VIVID bright colours ③branch detail ④ near-finish bright highlights ⑤don't rely on value difference alone; always bright colours that are vivid (発色の良い).
   Colours used: far mountain: cobalt blue, chrome green 3~sepia~black, heliotrope, emerald (+sky mixes). Mid rock: jaune brillant, french grey, blue celeste (+sky). Shrubs: olive green, cobalt violet, black, cobalt blue, sepia, heliotrope. Dark foliage: chrome green No.2, peacock blue. Grass part: jaune brillant, light green, emerald. Near rock: base + scarlet lake. Grass: cobalt green, chrome No.1, orange.
11 finish ③detail: raise rock texture; draw bright conspicuous LEAF SHAPES to make shrub contour stand out; shadow into branches tightens. Avoid adding strokes needlessly; choose points carefully & quickly; tighten where dark, vivid where wanted.
12 finish ④: near shrub leaves, branches, grass clumps, moss, flowers. ①sakuyo tip slightly splayed -> grass. ②moss on rock: 片ボカシ one-side blur (paint on tip only, blur with water at root). ③not photoreal but some reference research.
13 complete: petal shapes & leaf texture per reference; want "flower" (華) -> add more flowers as service. Check from distance, add intuitively where lacking. ~11h from ground laying.
The finished cover: vignette composition — sky & mist = bare paper-white area; peak dissolved; mass is a diagonal wedge rising to the right; point = flowering shrub right, rocks lit.
EOF

## Ch.2 cont. (p.96-108)
- 3-3 snow image board: lit snow basically WHITE; colour only in the shadows, and distance & space must be expressed there. Dense cedar grove far vs white snow on shrub branches -> contrast. Shade of snow volume (立体感の陰影): blue-grey family; shadows cast ON the snow surface: celeste family — two different shadow families. Scattered bounce from snow surface shown. Point: shrubs etc give tension.
- 3-4 C11-1 intrinsic vs light colour: backlit cherry tree near centre in shadow, contrast vs bright precinct. Color well used even in shadow. Every natural thing has its intrinsic colour, but visible colour changes with light: browns look greenish or bluish from scattered/indirect light. Old cherry actually black-brown~grey, but moss -> bluish. Dry moss on old trees & rocks goes whitish -> can use varied colours as if reflecting something. Trunk in shadow uses red-brown & green -> rich not lonely. Strong near shadow -> far nuke good. Multiplane: near cherry/branches/weeds on cels slid for parallax. Contrast strong because any overlap when moving must look right.
- 3-5 C2-2 evening sun into zoki woods: side light -> long shadows; ground matière complex -> ORGANISE, draw the point light & shadow, then not many strokes needed. Backlight easier: shape as silhouette, light easy to put. Front light: colour, detail, volume of lit object hard.
- 3-6 C63-3 light into hall: lit floor must still read as SAME PLANE as unlit floor; too much value/chroma difference -> lit part pops out, not same plane. Board seams/grain help; carpet/linoleum harder. Shadow angle natural else not floor shadow.
- 4 air: humidity & air. Low humidity -> good nuke, more depth. Hazy heat -> high humidity. Northern-born painters show little humidity, southern ones more. Location hunting to feel climate that photos can't.
- 4-1 C5-3 panorama: Oga (Akita-born) low-humidity clear air; pylon near-left for distance, but also: how things look at that distance, detail at distance, moonlit space colour. "Just paint it naturally as if it is there" — but needs everything: colour, contrast, detail degree, omission, exaggeration. Wide view, don't overdetail, power on points; orange machinery as one-point that makes distance.
- 4-2 C40-1 aerial perspective: dry winter -> Fuji clear. Far objects approach sky colour & melt, hazy = 空気遠近. Far mountains vs near snowfield -> big distance.
- 4-3 C24-1 humid: few light colours, value held down, object colours inside -> lustre in dark. Kanto loam red soil moist in shadow. C22-19 after-rain sky: cloud remnant vs clearing.
- 5 layout: art decides nature within layout; 5-1 C1-1 multi: 3-tier book of bamboo; dark bamboo near vs bright paddies far; shadow parts coloured so no lonely diagram. Far drawn from slightly above, near bamboo from front -> not-too-neat "zure" (offset) seen in natural vegetation -> composition not explanatory. Tanuki dispersed, melt into landscape.
- 5-2 C1-3 orthodox composition BG-only: seasonal plants (sunflower); weeds need care; house structures accurate -> scale.
- 5-3 C36-9: space for characters to act "alive" is the main purpose.
- 6 colour & space: learned from past paintings, Western, Nihonga, ink painting.
- 6-1 C1-2 scattering colour: many colours but unified; persimmon as point; far satoyama coloured trees each pale but purple-pink-orange widely; cedar chrome-green gives colour contrast. Near-right grass value NOT lowered -> keeps brightness; instead firm shadow along the path gives contrast.
- 6-2 C15-13 artificial: red-brown earth divides green near and pale blue sky; blue roof, red cone as accents to increase number of colours; aerial perspective for nuke; boundary grasses: withered-coloured weeds & sprouts.
- 6-3 C39-5 cold sad: narrow tone range, low saturation; no direct sun -> weak contrast -> flat risk; use GREYS: brown-grey, green-grey, purple-grey, red-grey, yellow-grey -> width equal to normal colours.
- 7 elegance & play: season, region, vegetation, weather is art's to decide; 7-1 cherry: real cherry season sky whitish; painted clear blue anyway. Cherry blossom rendered as flat opaque dabs of 3 values (white, lilac, blue-lilac) on blue with black branch strokes — broken, dashed lines.
- 7-2 moon: board reddish orange uncanny; final restrained white.
- 7-3 snow falling.
- Talk 7: Oga: BG must draw space & ground characters can move on. Kawai Gyokudō (Nihonga) nature + Monet; "Monet + Gyokudō / 2". Don't copy others' mixing; learn combos where colour lives in your own paint. Summer leaves hardest (all green); autumn many colours. Totoro green too bright/bluish (emerald); should have used deeper, red-brown-containing green. Omoide overcorrected; Pompoko: omission & narrowing points.
- 7-4..7-7 seasonal flowers as kigo (seasonal word): minimum objects convey season.

Write: books/oga/MODEL.md
# The model in Oga's book, in its own terms

*Kazuo Oga, 男鹿和雄画集Ⅱ (Ghibli THE ART series, Tokuma Shoten, 2005).*

Page numbers are the book's printed folios. The scan's image file for a page is `pages/p-NNN.jpg` with NNN = folio + 2 (so p.46 is `p-048.jpg`). The Japanese terms are the book's own; I translated them myself.

The book is in four parts:
- a step-by-step making of the cover painting (pp.4–11);
- Chapter 1, "Techniques of *Princess Mononoke*" (技法, pp.36–63);
- Chapter 2, "Expression in *Pompoko*" (表現, pp.88–108);
- a conversation between Oga and the art director Naoya Tanaka, plus two columns (pp.128–139).

Short "Oga × Tanaka" talks run through both chapters. The paintings are identified by cut number (C-0064 and so on).

The book never states its model as a list. It teaches by pairing a principle with one painting, often a board beside the final background. Below I set out that model in its own order and vocabulary, then give the rule underneath each part in one line (**Rule:**).

---

## 0. What a background is for

- **The background exists to give the characters a space and a ground they can live and move on** (舞台空間; p.101, and Oga in talk 7, p.106). "The biggest difference from other paintings is that you must firmly paint the space and the ground a character can move on."
- The workflow is storyboard → art setting in pencil (美術設定) → **art board** (美術ボード: Oga's colour study that fixes the image for the whole staff) → **background** (背景画: the same atmosphere as the board, finer in detail) (p.38–39). Over one scene, the board is the standard.
- **The cut's role sets how much work it gets** (時間と質と量, time, quality and quantity, p.58). A 2-second cut gets a quick, rough touch. A 7.5-second pan gets detail. A cut whose focus is an action (the falling tower, C-0049) gets deliberately rough scenery.

**Rule:** decide what the picture is for, and how long it will be looked at, before deciding how much to paint.

## 1. Tools and colour: few paints, mixed freely (pp.40–45)

- Poster colour (Nicker), mostly opaque. Oga uses 21 colours and the book lists 25 (p.41). The cover used 18 (p.4). "Even with many colours I couldn't master them. It's better to control a few colours freely" (talk 1). **A small palette, and a wide range from subtle mixing.**
- Opaque layered over opaque muddies. Transparent paints use the paper's white for vivid colour. Opaque on top suppresses an under colour. **Order and calculation of painting matter** (p.41).
- **Unmuddied mixing** (濁らない混色; C-0081, p.44):
  - high chroma and a big value range are held together by the **middle colours**: saturated olive, chrome green No.2, light green, *bridging* light and dark.
  - "If a big value difference is not joined by colours that keep their chroma, it goes muddy, for the same reason white plus black makes grey."
  - Near-shadow hues (cobalt green, chrome No.3) are **laid in unmixed** to keep saturation and give **lustre** (艶).
- **Not fully mixed** (混ざり切らない; C-0804, p.45): green and brown from the ground stage mingle without being fully mixed, so their saturation survives. Many unintended half-mixes stay vivid and give **transparency and depth**. The mossy trunk has a near-white chrome-green highlight, a vivid chrome No.1 accent around it, and chrome No.2 at the boundary to shadow.
- Browns are *mixed* from complements (vermilion + light green; cobalt violet + olive green → "natural grassy brown"). Greens tend to go cold, so warm them deliberately to widen the range (C-1651, p.43).
- **The same colour reads near or far by its place and role.** Put the sky-tinted colour of the far mountains on the side plane of a nearer mountain and a layer of air appears (talk 2, p.45). The farthest forest in *Totoro* is emerald and pale purple **not fully mixed**. A forest isn't simply green: bleed a little red or purple into it. "Scatter a few vivid colours well and it expresses light."

**Rule:** a small palette; the big value range bridged by saturated mid-tones; accents unmixed; many half-mixed colours beside one another.

## 2. The ground stage: 地塗り (pp.46–49, and cover steps 5–8)

"**Ground laying is the most important process in background painting**" (p.46). It's done on wet, stretched paper while the paper holds water, which gives 30 minutes to an hour.

- What it must settle: **the overall light direction, the overall colour, and the atmosphere of the subject** (p.48).
- Oga: "I try to bring the ground stage **as close to finished as possible**. In a landscape the sky is most of the area, the dark and bright parts of the forest, the sense of volume… get it good at the ground stage. Afterwards I just tidy the bright leaf tips in front and the rough parts" (talk 3, p.47). "Something to catch on is better than nothing for the finishing touches."
- Wet-in-wet effects are part of the method:
  - bleeding (にじみ): the edge of a bleed is read as moss, and scarlet lake scatters its particles when bled (C-1517);
  - blur (ぼかし);
  - the half-dry brush (半乾き), which "captures shape firmly".
- **C-0017** (p.47): a whole composition, from the far range to a stone-walled path, which is "ground-laid, with a little finish". "A good painting evokes a rich image **by its silhouette alone**." The enlarged middle forest isn't detailed but boldly blocked, and seen whole it looks natural.
- **C-0054** (p.49): a beech trunk and crown, with most of the texture put in with the flat brush at the ground stage. Up close there's colour melting and **uneven paint (塗りムラ)**, and **the unevenness reads as light and texture**.
- **Cover, steps 5–8** (pp.7–9):
  - The misty sky is **white over everything**, then French grey and blue celeste worked into it, "wrapped in mist, melting".
  - The far peak is painted into the wet sky with the sky's own colours. Its conifers are made by **the flat brush shifted vertically in a staggered way**, then one step darker layered for shade.
  - The alpine shrubs come from **a flat brush with its tip splayed on purpose** (バサつかせ). The brush direction changes, vertical and horizontal. The paint is a little thicker so the crowns don't lose to the colour beneath, and a darker colour goes in as shadow accent.
  - Fine branches and leaves are painted **already at the ground stage**, so they "blend with the colour under them, give unity of space, and save finishing".
  - At the end of the ground stage, "the space and contrast from far to near are already pinned; only texture remains, added without breaking the space."

**Rule:** the whole picture (light, colour, atmosphere, space, volume) exists at the lowest level of detail before any detail exists. Detail is a thin last layer that must not break it.

## 3. Grasp the crowd: 群れを捉える (C-1646, p.48)

- The hardest thing in nature painting is "**the rest, the great mass**" (その他大勢), where it's hard to pin down points.
- "If you draw the miscellany without organising it, it doesn't work as a background. Grasp the impression accurately, **organise** it, and then the painter **extracts** the features."
- "When drawing a crowd, don't draw everything in it. Drawing everything crushes the space and weakens the subject's impression."

**Rule:** a mass of like things is painted as one impression with a few extracted members, never member by member.

## 4. Coloured greys (p.48, p.57, p.103)

- Oga's trait: **grey used effectively**, never pure grey but purplish, brownish, greenish greys, "greys that feel coloured" (p.48). Overmixed, they become mud-grey. **Coloured greys scattered among the colours give vividness and lustre.**
- "Trees are basically grey, but not plain grey: greys of some hue, parts where the hue is strong, parts covered in moss." In light, add a bright grey or reflect the sky's colour into the tree's colour. In shadow, go dark **with the tree's own hue strengthened** (talk 4, p.57).
- **C39-5** (p.103): cold, overcast, sad scenes narrow the tone range and lower saturation, and without direct sun the contrast is weak and the picture risks going flat. Use greys: **brown-grey, green-grey, purple-grey, red-grey, yellow-grey**, "a range equal to the normal colours".
- Trunks are mostly grey (except red pine and Kurobe cedar), so **the width of the greys is the depth of the colour** (p.90).

**Rule:** neutrals are always tinted, several tints are used side by side, and in low light the greys carry the whole range of hue.

## 5. Living brushwork: 生きている筆使い (pp.50–51)

- "If the brush traces are alive, the painting is alive." A living brush form gives the **unity** nature needs.
- **Rhythm** (C-0799 moss): big colour and shade at the ground stage, then the tip of the sakuyo brush darting vertically and horizontally, **near-improvised**. "Real fine moss leaves are **not drawn one by one**." Tip, belly and root of the brush; sometimes a splayed tip for a rough touch. "For a crowd you must know **beforehand what shape each brush motion makes**. Practise on scraps."
- **Bold, unhesitating strokes** (C-1471, moonlit moss floor): moss, dead leaves and ferns are kept to a minimum. There's no focal point: only the moon's reflection and **a shadow crossing the whole composition** give the picture its tension.
- **Painting what isn't painted** (C-0822, the roots of a big tree): the moss, ferns and suckers floating in light are captured *only* by **the bright parts of leaves facing the light**, glinting red-brown stems and glowing moss heads. That "makes the unpainted parts felt… close to an illusion", and it takes an understanding of how nature is made. No repeated overpainting; the fewest strokes.
- **The hake** (C-1354, C-0801): gradations across a rock wall and water, and light shafts from the canopy, are laid with the wide hake at the ground stage. It grades two or more hues without raising the paint's density, so the colour stays bright. It's used to **emphasise a direction** in the picture.

**Rule:** a stroke is a known shape used many times at tempo, not a depiction of one leaf. In the light, a few lit marks stand for the whole unpainted mass.

## 6. Space and planes: 空間と面の意識 (pp.52–55)

Vocabulary:
- **抜け (nuke)**, the clearance or openness of a picture. With good nuke, space and perspective are clear and distance is felt; with bad nuke a landscape looks like a miniature.
- **面 (men)**, the plane. "Planes don't match" means no consistency. A character standing on a ground seen from above must match that plane or look tilted. Perspective can be correct and still look artificial: "for nature, over-caring about perspective loosens the space."

The plates:
- **C-0064** (p.52), board vs background: a slope with a distant village. **A ridge line cutting the slope midway**, and **the strong shadow of trees lit from the west**, divide the bright near meadow from the village far off. "**Intentionally severing connected space gives depth.**" The board scattered its points; the background **concentrates them** on the ridge trees. "If something clearly shaped exists somewhere, people recognise the space around it even if the surroundings are vague." Oga gives contrast to nature, which is hard to shape, to make the scale of the space.
- **C-0095** (p.53), ground as a plane: "the hardest is simple ground. Keep in mind a **flat plane with undulations, and grass growing on it**." Otherwise the grass doesn't fit the plane and scatters. The west sun shades the grass clumps and lights the undulations. Several grass types give broad variation, and shadows give contrast. "In a slightly looking-down composition, grass and leaves near are drawn **as seen from above**, far ones **more from the side**." Understand the flow and continuity.
- **C-0022** (p.53), the boundaries between lying planes (grass, ground) and rising planes (stone wall, forest). The whole picture is in shadow and is carried by the volume of the crowns and a glossy stone wall, **mastery of shadow colour**. One thin ray into the dark gives awe.
- **C-0773** (p.54–55), a great landscape: two ridges overlap with cloud passing between; the near rock wall, lit by morning sun, is the point; a small figure among shrubs gives scale.
  - It's pan-focus, so nothing can be blurred. Draw back to front firmly, and use **aerial perspective** (the far approaches the sky colour), **a difference of brush touch**, and **the placement of points**.
  - Eye point: trees above the eye point are drawn from below, trees below it from above. Errors in brush direction or placement break it.

**Rule:** depth is made by severing space (a ridge, a band of shadow) and by concentrating the points; the ground is one plane with undulations and its marks belong to that plane (top view near, side view far).

## 7. Grasping the light source: 光源の捉え方 (pp.56–57, pp.94–97)

- **Set the source consciously**: season, weather and time change the same place (p.56).
- **The sun's colour** (C-0772): a white sun alone doesn't look like light, so add the light's **colour**, above all **where light meets an object's edge** (際):
  - the celeste sky's boundary pale pink; light blue melting into emerald; a flare of pale orange and yellow, prism-like;
  - the far range grading yellow → emerald; the near, directly lit, yellow → orange; chrome green No.1 and light green vivid at the edges of the light;
  - blue and brown greys inside the white-lit cloud for depth.

  Full backlight, and still rich colour.
- **Light and shadow** (C-0792, gorge boulders):
  - Light cuts into the ravine, and **shadow is about 2/3 of the picture**, which makes the light dazzle on the rocks. Light falls only on certain planes; the upper cliff is in shadow. "**Don't spread the light around; concentrate it in one part.**"
  - There are three light levels: direct sun brightest, soft-lit parts, and **reflected light** in the shadow, dim but important.
  - By day the reflected light takes the sky's colour, so it's cool. Alone that's too cold, so add the object's own colour (rock browns, plant greens) to loosen it. "If the shadow reads as shadow compared to the light, warm colours in the shadow are fine", and that widens the range of light and colour.
- **Moonlight, two ways** (C-1482 vs C-1486): airbrush for light in the air itself (fantastical; don't raise its value too high or it goes whitish), or brush alone, where **shadows cast between trees express the light**: crisp, sharp-edged light. "If you don't want the light to diffuse, don't airbrush."
- **Shadows aren't just lower value** (talk 4): "background painters tend to make shadow by lowering value; Oga puts browns in the shadow." Miyazaki: "this cedar could be blacker." Oga admits he tends to paint as he sees, with not enough exaggeration.
- **Pompoko** (pp.94–97):
  - Tilted or limited light gives strong contrast, and **the light's colour enters the shadow**, which sets the dominant tone.
  - **Dusk** (C58-1A): warm light against cool shadow, **purple between blue and pink**, in harmonious combinations, or the colour muddies.
  - **Snow** (board, p.96): lit snow is basically **white**; colour goes only in the shadows, and distance and space must be expressed there. The snow's own shading (its volume) is blue-grey; shadows cast *on* the snow surface are celeste. **Two shadow families.** Scattered bounce from the snow is shown.
  - **Intrinsic colour vs light colour** (C11-1): every natural thing has its own colour, but browns look greenish or bluish under scattered and indirect light. An old cherry, black-brown to grey, is painted bluish because of moss; dry moss on old trees and rocks goes whitish and can take varied colours as if it reflects something. A trunk in shadow gets red-brown and green: "rich, not lonely". **A strong near shadow makes the far nuke good.**
  - **Low side sun into woods** (C2-2): the long shadows and complex ground are **organised**, then the point light and shadow are painted, and after that few strokes are needed. Backlight is easier (shapes as silhouettes, light easy to place). Front light is hard (colour, detail and volume of the lit object).
  - **Light on a floor** (C63-3): the lit floor must read as **the same plane** as the unlit floor. Too much difference in value or chroma and the lit part pops out. Shadow angles must be natural or they stop reading as the floor's.

**Rule:** one light, concentrated; shadow is the majority and is full of colour (cool reflected light plus the object's warm colour, at the widest range that still reads as shadow); the light's colour lives at its edges.

## 8. Balance and density: 画面のバランスと密度 (pp.58–59)

- "Density doesn't come from drawing-in alone. Cover everything with fine detail and it's just 'a fine picture'."
- **Narrow down the points**, where the eye will look. "The viewer looks first at the points; the vague parts get completed by the viewer. **Concentrate density at the points and the whole feels dense.** Scatter the points and the density thins."
- C-0027 and C-0051 (2 s and 2.5 s cuts): the contrast of a west-lit village against a mountain's shadow; the detail isn't fine; part is left at the ground stage.
- C-0049 (4.5 s): rough, quick touches, because the focus is the falling tower.
- Talk (p.134): "Density isn't about filling… find the **conditions under which the picture holds without detail**, place them well, and a different density is born." *Pompoko* settled its points by season: which flowers bloom, what state the grass is in.

**Rule:** density is a property of the points, not of the area. Choose them (few), give them the detail, and let the rest hold at ground level.

## 9. Omission and exaggeration: 省略と誇張 (デフォルメ) (pp.60–61)

- Narrowed points, omitted and exaggerated, look rough but have **tension**. The information must be **organised**, or omission looks like cutting corners.
- **C-0007** (p.60), board vs background: the board's strong west-sun orange and long shadows fall on the distant trees too, so there's little depth. The background moves to earlier in the afternoon and **softens the shadows' contrast going back**, so perspective appears. The near shrubs are in clear detail; the far ones are omitted. "Bringing the point to the front makes it possible to omit the rest."
- **C-0025** (p.61): the tower and the far mountains overlap; the middle mountains are split left and right; a shadow runs from centre front to the back. The staging has priorities (見得, a kabuki "pose").
- **C-0827** (p.61), close broadleaf evergreens, "**omission after capturing the features**": **form, colour and light are the three elements**. Real foliage is chaotic; organised foliage keeps its volume and a dynamic space. "It isn't an encyclopedia." Capture the whole impression of colour, form and light broadly, *then* add texture.
- Observation isn't carelessness (適当). Omission is a decision; carelessness isn't (p.89).

**Rule:** omission is the other half of the points: keep form, colour and light, drop the members, and let contrast soften with distance.

## 10. Location reference and research (pp.62–63, p.137)

- **Shadow colour** (C-0781, p.62): in shadow the tone range narrows, and the picture goes lonely and poor. **Use the widest range of colours that still reads as shadow.** For a moist shadow, don't rely on cool colours only: use the object's natural warm colours, from the memory of the air, texture, vegetation and weather on location.
- **Stone wall in shadow** (C-0008): at the ground stage, thin low-value paint lets particles float on the paper and gives a rough texture. Then opaque colour goes on firmly: a varied texture.
- **Reference** (C-0053, p.63): photos are hints, never copied into the space or composition. The red-barked Kurobe cedar is a one-point accent.
- "Just paint it naturally, **as if it is there**" (それがそこにあるように自然に, p.98). But that takes everything together: colour, contrast, degree of detail, omission, exaggeration.

## 11. Air: 空気感 (pp.98–99)

- **Humidity and air**: low humidity gives good nuke and more depth; hazy heat means high humidity.
- **C5-3** (p.98): the moonlit panorama in Oga's own low-humidity air. What makes the space is how things look at that distance, the detail at that distance, and the colour of moonlit space. The wide view isn't overdetailed, and the orange construction machinery is the one point that creates the distance.
- **C40-1**, aerial perspective (空気遠近, p.99): far objects approach the sky's colour and melt into it.
- **C24-1**, humid woods: few light colours, value held down, the objects' colours kept inside it, so there's **lustre in the dark**.

## 12. Layout and composition (pp.100–101)

- **C1-1** (p.100): a three-layer "book" of bamboo in front, dark, against bright paddies far. The shadow parts are coloured, so it never becomes a lonely diagram. The far is drawn from slightly above, the near bamboo from the front, which gives the not-too-neat **offset (ずれ)** of natural vegetation, "so the composition doesn't become explanatory".
- The picture holds as BG-only; seasonal plants and accurate structures give scale.

## 13. Colour and space (pp.102–103)

- **C1-2**, scattering colour: many colours, unified. The persimmon is the point. The far satoyama trees are each pale but spread widely through purple, pink and orange; the cedar's chrome green gives colour contrast. **The near grass's value is *not* lowered, so the brightness holds; a firm shadow along the path gives the contrast instead.**
- **C15-13**: the red earth divides the green near from the pale blue sky. A blue roof and red cones are accents, to add colours.

## 14. Elegance and play: 風流さと遊び心 (pp.104–108)

- Season, region, vegetation and weather are the art department's to decide. A **seasonal flower works as a kigo** (a season word in a haiku): a minimum of objects conveys the season.
- **C17-3**, cherry: the real sky in cherry season is whitish, but it's painted clear blue anyway. The blossom is **flat opaque dabs in three values (white, lilac, blue-lilac)** on the blue, with broken black branch strokes.

## 15. The working order, from the cover (pp.4–11)

1. Pencil drawing directly on the paper; stretch the paper (水張り).
2. Mix only as needed; control by the amount of paint and water on the brush.
3. **Ground stage, far to near**: sky (white, then greys) → far peak in the wet (the staggered flat brush) → middle rocks → near vegetation (the splayed flat brush, strokes vertical and horizontal) → fine branches and leaves in the ground stage.
4. Let it dry for 1–2 hours.
5. **Finish 1**: normally far to near, but first put **one deliberately strong touch** on the middle rocks, to read the balance of colour and contrast. Then **add only where it's weak**, "so at least you never overdraw".
6. **Finish 2**, bright and dark: lustrous deep colours in the shrub and rock shadows. The dark tightens the picture, so the bright parts now look lacking; add **vivid** brights. Then branch detail and highlights. "**Don't rely on value difference alone; always use bright colours that sing** (発色の良い)."
7. **Finish 3–4**: rock texture; **bright, conspicuous leaf shapes to make the shrub's contour stand out**; shadow into the branches. Grass with a slightly splayed sakuyo; moss with a one-sided blur (片ボカシ). Reference for the species.
8. **Finish 5**: flower petals; "if the picture wants a 'flower' (華), add more flowers as a service". Step back, check from a distance, add intuitively where something is lacking. About 11 hours from the ground stage.

The cover's palette by passage (p.10):

| Passage | Colours |
|---|---|
| far mountain | cobalt blue, chrome green No.3 to sepia to black, heliotrope, emerald (plus sky mixes) |
| middle rock | jaune brillant, French grey, blue celeste (plus sky) |
| shrubs | olive, cobalt violet, black, cobalt blue, sepia, heliotrope |
| dark foliage | chrome green No.2, peacock blue |
| grass area | jaune brillant, light green, emerald |
| near rock | the base plus scarlet lake |
| grass | cobalt green, chrome green No.1, orange |

## 16. Things said in passing that bind

- "**Look and steal**" (見て盗む) is the whole method of learning (p.129).
- Oga's mixing is a cook's, pinch by pinch; "don't copy others' mixes; learn the combinations in which colour comes alive in your own paint" (p.106).
- Summer leaves are the hardest (all green); autumn is easy. *Totoro*'s green was too bright and bluish (emerald); it should have been a deeper green with red-brown in it (p.107).
- "Monet plus Kawai Gyokudō, divided by two" (p.106).
- "Even when it isn't working, a big brush and a bold correction often beats nursing it" (p.132). A washed-out failure once left beautiful residual colour.
- The column on the airbrush (p.137): blowing the same colour back comes out whitish, because the particles float, so blow a slightly higher chroma.

---

## The model in one paragraph

A background is the ground and space for a life. Settle light, colour and atmosphere first, at the lowest level of detail, as nearly finished as possible (地塗り). Make depth by severing space and concentrating the points (抜け, 面). Keep one light, concentrated. Shadow takes most of the picture and holds its widest range of colour: cool reflected light, the object's warm colour, the light's colour at the edges. Hold the big value range together with saturated mid-tones and unmixed accents. Tint every grey. Paint crowds as organised impressions with extracted members, using known brush shapes at tempo, and in the light let a few lit marks stand for the unpainted mass. Put density only at the points. Omit the rest by keeping form, colour and light, and soften contrast with distance. Then add detail thinly, and only where it's weak.

"""OKLab / OKLCh helpers (numpy). Arrays of shape (...,3), sRGB in 0..1."""
import numpy as np


def srgb_to_lin(c):
    c = np.asarray(c, np.float64)
    return np.where(c <= 0.04045, c / 12.92, ((c + 0.055) / 1.055) ** 2.4)


def lin_to_srgb(c):
    c = np.clip(c, 0, 1)
    return np.where(c <= 0.0031308, c * 12.92, 1.055 * c ** (1 / 2.4) - 0.055)


_M1 = np.array([[0.4122214708, 0.5363325363, 0.0514459929],
                [0.2119034982, 0.6806995451, 0.1073969566],
                [0.0883024619, 0.2817188376, 0.6299787005]])
_M2 = np.array([[0.2104542553, 0.7936177850, -0.0040720468],
                [1.9779984951, -2.4285922050, 0.4505937099],
                [0.0259040371, 0.7827717662, -0.8086757660]])


def rgb_to_oklab(rgb):
    lms = srgb_to_lin(rgb) @ _M1.T
    return np.cbrt(lms) @ _M2.T


def oklab_to_rgb(lab):
    lms_ = np.asarray(lab) @ np.linalg.inv(_M2).T
    return lin_to_srgb((lms_ ** 3) @ np.linalg.inv(_M1).T)


def lab_to_lch(lab):
    L, a, b = lab[..., 0], lab[..., 1], lab[..., 2]
    return np.stack([L, np.hypot(a, b), np.arctan2(b, a)], -1)


def lch_to_lab(lch):
    L, C, h = lch[..., 0], lch[..., 1], lch[..., 2]
    return np.stack([L, C * np.cos(h), C * np.sin(h)], -1)


def hexrgb(h):
    h = h.lstrip('#')
    return np.array([int(h[i:i + 2], 16) for i in (0, 2, 4)]) / 255.0


def u8(rgb):
    return (np.clip(rgb, 0, 1) * 255 + 0.5).astype(np.uint8)
Write: books/oga/src/measure.py
"""Measure Oga's plates (and photos / renders) for the book's claims.

  python3 measure.py img1 img2 ...   -> table of numbers

Every image is first area-reduced to 320 px wide, the size we paint at, so all numbers are
comparable across plates, photos and renders.

Numbers (all in OKLab):
  shadow%   share of pixels in the dark family (Otsu split of L)      [book: shadow ~2/3, p.56]
  Llit/Lsh  mean L of lit family / shadow family
  Csh/Clit  mean chroma of shadow family / lit family
  hue_sh    circular hue spread (0..1) in the shadow family: how many hues live in the shadow
  abSD_sh   std of (a,b) in shadow / in lit                              [book: widest range in shadow, p.62]
  grey%     share of low-chroma pixels (C<.04) ; pureGrey = share of those with C<.012
  tints     number of 45-degree hue sectors holding >=10% of the low-chroma pixels [coloured greys, p.48]
  midC      chroma of the mid-L third / mean chroma of top and bottom thirds    [bridging mid-tones, p.44]
  dens20    share of fine-detail energy in the busiest 20% of 16x16 blocks     [density at points, p.58]
"""
import sys
import numpy as np
from PIL import Image
sys.path.insert(0, __file__.rsplit('/', 1)[0])
from okl import rgb_to_oklab


def load320(path, w=320):
    im = Image.open(path).convert('RGB')
    h = max(1, round(im.height * w / im.width))
    return np.asarray(im.resize((w, h), Image.BOX), np.float64) / 255.0


def otsu(x, bins=128):
    h, e = np.histogram(x, bins=bins)
    c = (e[:-1] + e[1:]) / 2
    w0 = np.cumsum(h); w1 = w0[-1] - w0
    m0 = np.cumsum(h * c) / np.maximum(w0, 1)
    m1 = (np.sum(h * c) - np.cumsum(h * c)) / np.maximum(w1, 1)
    return c[np.argmax(w0 * w1 * (m0 - m1) ** 2)]


def circ_spread(h, w=None):
    w = np.ones_like(h) if w is None else w
    R = np.hypot((w * np.cos(h)).sum(), (w * np.sin(h)).sum()) / max(w.sum(), 1e-9)
    return 1 - R


def stats(rgb):
    lab = rgb_to_oklab(rgb)
    L, a, b = lab[..., 0], lab[..., 1], lab[..., 2]
    C = np.hypot(a, b); H = np.arctan2(b, a)
    t = otsu(L)
    sh = L < t; lt = ~sh
    out = {}
    out['shadow%'] = 100 * sh.mean()
    out['Llit'] = L[lt].mean(); out['Lsh'] = L[sh].mean()
    out['Csh'] = C[sh].mean(); out['Clit'] = C[lt].mean()
    out['hue_sh'] = circ_spread(H[sh], C[sh])
    out['abSD_sh'] = np.sqrt(a[sh].var() + b[sh].var())
    out['abSD_lit'] = np.sqrt(a[lt].var() + b[lt].var())
    g = C < 0.04
    out['grey%'] = 100 * g.mean()
    out['pureGrey'] = 100 * (C[g] < 0.012).mean() if g.any() else 0
    if g.sum() > 20:
        sec = ((H[g] + np.pi) / (np.pi / 4)).astype(int) % 8
        share = np.bincount(sec, minlength=8) / g.sum()
        out['tints'] = int((share >= 0.10).sum())
    else:
        out['tints'] = 0
    q1, q2 = np.quantile(L, [1 / 3, 2 / 3])
    mid = C[(L >= q1) & (L < q2)].mean()
    ends = 0.5 * (C[L < q1].mean() + C[L >= q2].mean())
    out['midC'] = mid / max(ends, 1e-9)
    # fine-detail energy: |L - 3x3 box blur|
    from scipy.ndimage import uniform_filter
    hf = np.abs(L - uniform_filter(L, 3))
    Hh, Ww = (L.shape[0] // 16) * 16, (L.shape[1] // 16) * 16
    blk = hf[:Hh, :Ww].reshape(Hh // 16, 16, Ww // 16, 16).sum((1, 3)).ravel()
    blk = np.sort(blk)[::-1]
    out['dens20'] = 100 * blk[:max(1, len(blk) // 5)].sum() / blk.sum()
    return out


COLS = ['shadow%', 'Llit', 'Lsh', 'Csh', 'Clit', 'hue_sh', 'abSD_sh', 'abSD_lit', 'grey%', 'pureGrey',
        'tints', 'midC', 'dens20']


def table(paths, names=None):
    rows = []
    for i, p in enumerate(paths):
        s = stats(load320(p))
        rows.append((names[i] if names else p.rsplit('/', 1)[-1][:28], s))
    hdr = f"{'image':30s}" + ''.join(f'{c:>9s}' for c in COLS)
    lines = [hdr]
    for n, s in rows:
        lines.append(f'{n:30s}' + ''.join(f'{s[c]:9.3f}' if isinstance(s[c], float) else f'{s[c]:9d}' for c in COLS))
    return '\n'.join(lines), rows


if __name__ == '__main__':
    print(table(sys.argv[1:])[0])

def row(p):

    t=otsu(L); sh=L<t

# --- levels calibration: the scan's black point is ~L .30 (text black), paper white slightly tinted.
import numpy as np
def page_levels(i):
    a=np.asarray(Image.open(f'pages/p-{i:03d}.jpg').convert('RGB'),float)[::2,::2].reshape(-1,3)
    s=a.sum(1); o=np.argsort(s)
    k=np.median(a[o[:max(50,len(o)//1000)]],0); w=np.median(a[o[-len(o)//100:]],0)
    return k,w
for k_,(i,a,b,c,d) in P.items():
    k,w=page_levels(i)
    im=np.asarray(Image.open(f'plates/{k_}.png'),float)
    # linearise-ish: do levels in sRGB, then gentle so mid tones keep; clip
    out=np.clip((im-k)/(w-k),0,1)
    Image.fromarray((out*255+.5).astype(np.uint8)).save(f'plates/lv/{k_}.png')
    print(k_, 'black',k.round(0),'white',w.round(0))
EOF
